Encoding of the CK machine in HOcore

6.2.
Bisimilarities

To internalize normal-form bisimilarity,
we distinguish free variables
f
from bound variables
x
, as in
Section
4
. We assume
\lambda
-terms to be
well-formed, so that every bound variable has its binder.

t,s\Coloneqq f\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;x\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\lambda x.t\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;t\;s\qquad v\Coloneqq f\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\lambda x.t

The evaluation of an open term may result either in a value in an
empty stack, or in a
stuck term
of the form

\langle f\;\bullet\mathop{::}\pi,v\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}
: because the function is a free
variable, the
\beta
-reduction cannot be triggered. Normal-form
bisimilarity should be defined on these two kinds of normal forms.

For values, a first possibility is to proceed as in call-by-name,
i.e., to equate identical free variables, and to relate the bodies of

\lambda
-abstractions. This variant (discussed in
Remark
20
) is too discriminating in the plain

\lambda
-calculus: e.g., it distinguishes a free variable
f
from
its
\eta
-expansion
\lambda x.f\;x
although these terms are
contextually equivalent in call-by-value. Lassen
[
Las05
]

proposes instead to compare values by applying them to a fresh
variable. For instance, applying
f
and
\lambda x.f\;x
to
a fresh variable
f^{\prime}
produces the same term
f\;f^{\prime}
on
both sides after evaluation.

Normal-form bisimilarity compares two stuck terms

\langle f\;\bullet\mathop{::}\pi,v\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}
and

\langle f\;\bullet\mathop{::}{\pi^{\prime}},v^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}
by relating
v
and
v^{\prime}
, and
by testing
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
with a fresh variable
f^{\prime}
. To reflect
this test on stacks, we define normal-form bisimilarity not on terms,
but on configurations.

{defi}

A symmetric relation
\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}
on configurations is a normal-form
bisimulation if
C\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}C^{\prime}
implies:

•

if
C\mathrel{{\mapsto}^{*}}\langle[],v\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}
, then there exists
v^{\prime}
such
that
C^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapsto}^{*}}\langle[],v^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}
and for all fresh
f
, we
have

\langle v\;\bullet\mathop{::}[],f\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}\langle v^{\prime}\;\bullet\mathop{::}[],f\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}
;

•

if
C\mathrel{{\mapsto}^{*}}\langle f\;\bullet\mathop{::}\pi,v\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}
, then
there exist
\pi^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
such that

C^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapsto}^{*}}\langle f\;\bullet\mathop{::}\pi^{\prime},v^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}
, and for all
fresh
f^{\prime}
, we have

\langle v\;\bullet\mathop{::}[],f^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}\langle v^{\prime}\;\bullet\mathop{::}[],f^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}
and

\langle\pi,f^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}\langle\pi^{\prime},f^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}
.

Normal-form bisimilarity
\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{nf}}}
, is the largest normal-form bisimulation.

In both clauses, we compare values by considering configurations of
the form
\langle v\;\bullet\mathop{::}[],f\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}
, which corresponds to the
application
v\;f
, as wished. Normal-form bisimilarity is
extended to terms so that
t\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{nf}}}s
if

\langle t,[]\rangle\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{nf}}}\langle s,[]\rangle
. This equivalence can be related to
Böhm tree equivalence up to infinite
\eta
expansion through a
continuation passing style transformation
[
Las05
]
. It is
not complete w.r.t. the contextual equivalence of the call-by-value

\lambda
-calculus
[
Las05
, Example 3.2]
.

We internalize applicative bisimilarity as in call-by-name
(Section
5
), by considering a

\lambda
-calculus with de Bruijn indices and an environment-based CK
machine, i.e., the CEK abstract
machine
[
FF86
,
FFF09
]
. The
definition of the bisimilarity differs from call-by-name only in the
fact that the testing argument must be a

\lambda
-abstraction. Consequently, we just use the
argument-generating rules from Figure
4
on top of the
CEK machine, and change the rule
Restart
so that it can
trigger only if the constructed term is a

\lambda
-abstraction. Internalizing contextual equivalence in
call-by-value simply consists in adding the unmodified rules for
generating a context (Section
5.4
) on top of the CEK
machine. In the rest of this section, we discuss only the more
interesting case of the NFB machine and its translation in HOcore.

6.3.
NFB Machine

We extend the CK machine into a NFB machine in
Figure
8
. Configurations include a counter
n
to
generate fresh variables, as in call-by-name. The first four machine
steps correspond to the CK machine, while the last three ones compare
normal forms.

\displaystyle\langle t\;s,\pi,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle t,\bullet\;s\mathop{::}\pi,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Fun
)

\displaystyle\langle v,\pi,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle\pi,v,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

(
Switch
)

\displaystyle\langle\bullet\;t\mathop{::}\pi,v,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle t,v\;\bullet\mathop{::}\pi,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Arg
)

\displaystyle\langle\lambda x.t\;\bullet\mathop{::}\pi,v,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle[v\mathbin{/}x]t,\pi,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Beta
)

\displaystyle\langle[],v,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\ocirc$}}}\langle v\;\bullet\mathop{::}[],n,n+1\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

(
Val
)

\displaystyle\langle f\;\bullet\mathop{::}\pi,v,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$f$}}}\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\filledmedtriangledown$}}}\langle v\;\bullet\mathop{::}[],n,n+1\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

(
Stuck-Val
)

\displaystyle\langle f\;\bullet\mathop{::}\pi,v,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$f$}}}\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\filledmedtriangleright$}}}\langle\pi,n,n+1\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

(
Stuck-Context
)

Figure 8.
NFB machine for call by value

If we get a value, we flag
\ocirc
and apply it to a fresh variable
(transition
Val
). In the case of a stuck term

\langle f\;\bullet\mathop{::}\pi,v\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}
, we have to choose whether we test

v
(transition
Stuck-Val
) or
\pi
(transition

Stuck-Context
). In both cases, we flag the free
variable
f
. The test for values is the same as in the transition

Val
. If we choose the stack
\pi
, we simply restart the
machine in continuation mode with
\pi
and a fresh variable.

We prove that the resulting machine equivalence coincides with
normal-form bisimilarity using the same proof technique as in
call-by-name.

Theorem 19
.

t\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{nf}}}s
iff there exists
n>\max{(\mathsf{fv}(t)\cup\mathsf{fv}(s))}
such
that
\langle t,[],n\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{m}}}\langle s,[],n\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}
.

Remark 20
.

In extensions of the
\lambda
-calculus where functions are not the
only values (e.g., with booleans or integers as primitive
constructs), a value is no longer equivalent to its
\eta
-expansion
in general, and we would have to consider the finer normal-form
bisimilarity which distinguishes values based on their kind. To
internalize it in the plain
\lambda
-calculus, we would replace

Val
with the transitions
Done
and

Lambda
below, and
Stuck-Val
with the new step
below.

\displaystyle\langle[],f,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$f$}}}\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\medstar$}}}

(
Done
)

\displaystyle\langle[],\lambda x.t,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\ocirc$}}}\langle[n\mathbin{/}x]t,[],n+1\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Lambda
)

\displaystyle\langle f\;\bullet\mathop{::}\pi,v,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$f$}}}\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\filledmedtriangledown$}}}\langle[],v,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

(
Stuck-Val
)

If we get a free variable, we signal it and we are done. We
instantiate
\lambda
-abstractions with a fresh variable. Finally,
the transition
Stuck-Val
goes to
\langle[],v,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}
,
after which we immediately apply either
Done
or

Lambda
depending on
v
.

We present the encoding of the call-by-value NFB machine in
Figure
9
. We follow the same principles as in
Section
4.3
. For example, we still use an output on

b
to describe what to do when the stack is empty—run the

\mathsf{Restart}
process; the message on
b
is simply discarded when the
stack is not empty. The
\mathsf{Restart}
process flags
\ocirc
and then
recreates the configuration where the value in
val
is applied to a
fresh variable, represented by the counter
n
stored in
k
.

\displaystyle\left\ldbrack t\;s\right\rdbrack

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}c(p).(\left\ldbrack t\right\rdbrack\parallel\overline{c}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}}})

\displaystyle\left\ldbrack v\right\rdbrack

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}c(p).(p\parallel\overline{val}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}}}\parallel\overline{b}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}}})

\displaystyle\left\ldbrack\lambda x.t\right\rdbrack_{\mathsf{v}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}val(x).b(\_).\left\ldbrack t\right\rdbrack

\displaystyle\left\ldbrack f\right\rdbrack_{\mathsf{v}}

\displaystyle+\filledmedtriangleright(\_).(P_{\pi}\parallel\overline{val}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}}}\parallel\overline{k}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}}}\parallel\overline{b}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scripts \displaystyle\left\ldbrack\bullet\;t\mathop{::}\pi\right\rdbrack

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}\mathsf{Arg}(\left\ldbrack t\right\rdbrack,\left\ldbrack\pi\right\rdbrack)

\displaystyle\left\ldbrack v\;\bullet\mathop{::}\pi\right\rdbrack

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}\mathsf{Fun}(\left\ldbrack v\right\rdbrack_{\mathsf{v}},\left\ldbrack\pi\right\rdbrack)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Arg}(P_{t},P_{\pi})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}val(x).b(\_).(P_{t}\parallel\overline{c}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}}})

\displaystyle\mathsf{Fun}(P_{v},P_{\pi})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}P_{v}\parallel\overline{c}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}}}

\displaystyle\left\ldbrack\langle t,\pi,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}\right\rdbrack

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}\left\ldbrack t\right\rdbrack\parallel\overline{c}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}}}\parallel\overline{k}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}}}

\displaystyle\left\ldbrack\langle\pi,v,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}\right\rdbrack

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}{\left\ldbrack\pi\right\rdbrack}\parallel\overline{val}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}}}\parallel\overline{k}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}}}\parallel\overline{b}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}}}

Figure 9.
Encoding of the call-by-value NFB machine into HOcore

A free variable
f
is observable in a stuck configuration

\langle f\;\bullet\mathop{::}\pi,v,n\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}
; in that case, it signals
itself with a sequence of inputs on
suc
ended by an input on
z
,
and then it non-deterministically chooses between testing
v
or

\pi
. The
\mathsf{Chce}(P_{v},P_{\pi},P_{n})
process is making that
choice, applying
P_{n}
to
P_{v}
in the first case, or running
P_{n}
in

P_{\pi}
in the second one. Unlike in call-by-name, we do not have to
go recursively through the stack: as a result, although the CK machine
is more complex than the KAM, the translation of the call-by-value NFB
machine is arguably simpler than the call-by-name one.

We can prove full abstraction between the call-by-value NFB machines
and their translated HOcore processes as in
Section
4.4
, from which we can deduce full
abstraction between HOcore and the
\lambda
-calculus with
normal-form bisimilarity.

Theorem 21
.

t\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{nf}}}s
iff there exists
n>\max{(\mathsf{fv}(t)\cup\mathsf{fv}(s))}
such that

\left\ldbrack\langle t,[],n\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}\right\rdbrack\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle{HO}}}}\left\ldbrack\langle s,[],n\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}\right\rdbrack
.

7.
Control Operators

The benefit of using abstract machines as an intermediary step is that
our encoding can easily be extended to control operators, the
semantics of which can be expressed using abstract machines. Among the
existing control operators, we choose to encode the

\lambda\mu
-calculus
[
Par92
]
, to show how to deal with a calculus
with multiple binders.

7.1.
Extended KAM for the Call-by-Name

\lambda\mu
-calculus

The
\lambda\mu
-calculus extends the
\lambda
-calculus with

names
or
continuation variables
—ranged over by
\alpha

and
\beta
—which represent an unknown continuation, and a
\mu

operator to capture continuations. The syntax of terms becomes as
follows.

t,s\Coloneqq x\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\lambda x.t\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;t\;s\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\mu\alpha.[\beta]t

The semantics of the
\lambda\mu
-calculus is not defined on plain
terms but on
named terms
of the form
[\alpha]t
. In

\mu\alpha.[\beta]t
, the occurrences of
\alpha
in
[\beta]t

are bound. We write
\mathsf{fn}(t)
and
\mathsf{fn}([\alpha]t)
for the set of
free names of a term or named term. A term is closed if it has no free
variable and no free name. A named term
[\alpha]t
cannot be closed
as it has at least
\alpha
among its free names.

We define the semantics of the
\lambda\mu
-calculus using an
extension of the KAM, meaning that the continuations captured by the

\mu
operator are represented by stacks. Like in the call-by-name

\lambda
-calculus, a stack represents a sequence of applications of
terms; the difference is that it is ended by a name which represents
the toplevel in which the term is executed.

\pi\Coloneqq t\mathop{::}\pi\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\alpha

\displaystyle\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle\alpha

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}\pi

\displaystyle\alpha\{t\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}[\alpha]t

\displaystyle\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle\beta

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}\beta\mbox{ if }\alpha\neq\beta

\displaystyle(s\mathop{::}\pi)\{t\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}\pi\{t\;s\}

\displaystyle\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle x

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}x

\displaystyle\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle\lambda x.t

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}\lambda x.\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle t

\displaystyle\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle(t\;s)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle t\;\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle s

\displaystyle\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle\mu\beta_{1}.[\beta_{2}]t

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}\mu{\beta_{1}}.(\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle\beta_{2})\{\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle t\}

Figure 10.
Structural Substitution

The term
\mu\alpha.[\beta]t
captures such a stack
\pi
to replace

\alpha
in
[\beta]t
with it.
Structural
substitution

[
AH08
]

\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle t

produces a plain term where the name
\alpha
is replaced by
\pi
in

t
. It is defined in Figure
10
, alongside two
auxiliary operations: name substitution
\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle\alpha

which produces a stack, and plugging
\pi\{t\}
which produces a
named term. Plugging simply reconstructs around
t
the applications
represented by the stack
\pi
. In the case

\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle\mu\beta_{1}.[\beta_{2}]t
, we assume the free
name
\alpha
to be distinct from
\beta_{1}
, which is always possible
using
\alpha
-conversion. The captured stack
\pi
is restored when

\beta_{2}=\alpha
, as we plug
\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle t
inside
\pi
.

The semantics is given by the extended KAM, defined on the same
configurations as in Section
3
.

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\langle t,\pi\rangle

(configurations)

\displaystyle\langle t\;s,\pi\rangle

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle t,s\mathop{::}\pi\rangle

(
Push
)

\displaystyle\langle\lambda x.t,s\mathop{::}\pi\rangle

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle[s\mathbin{/}x]t,\pi\rangle

(
Grab
)

\displaystyle\langle\mu\alpha.[\beta]t,\pi\rangle

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle t,\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle\beta\rangle

(
Capture
)

The
Push
and
Grab
steps are unchanged. The last
step captures the stack
\pi
and replaces
\alpha
with it, thanks to
the operations defined in Figure
10
. To evaluate a
closed term, we assume the existence of a distinguished name
\mathsf{tp}

representing the toplevel
[
AH08
]
, and we execute

\langle t,\mathsf{tp}\rangle
.

{exa}

Unlike the
\mu
operator, the control operator call/cc from Scheme
leaves the captured stack in place when it triggers. This behaviour
can be encoded in the
\lambda\mu
-calculus as follows
[
Par92
]
:

\mathsf{callcc}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}\lambda x.\mu\alpha.[\alpha]x\;\lambda y.\mu\beta.[\alpha]y

The captured stack is immediately restored, but also saved in the
value passed to
x
. To illustrate the extended KAM, we run it on an
example. Let
id\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}\lambda x.x
,
\Omega\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}(\lambda x.x\;x)\;(\lambda x.x\;x)
,
v\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}\lambda z.z\;id\;\Omega
, and
t
be any term. We compute
\mathsf{callcc}\;v\;t

in the name
\mathsf{tp}
.

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{callcc}\;v\;t,\mathsf{tp}\rangle

\displaystyle\mapsto^{2}\langle\mathsf{callcc},v\mathop{::}t\mathop{::}\mathsf{tp}\rangle

(
Push
-

Push
)

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle\mu\alpha.[\alpha]v\;\lambda y.\mu\beta.[\alpha]y,t\mathop{::}\mathsf{tp}\rangle

(
grab
)

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle v\;\lambda y.\mu\beta.[\mathsf{tp}]y\;t,t\mathop{::}\mathsf{tp}\rangle

(
Capture
)

\displaystyle\mapsto^{2}\langle(\lambda y.\mu\beta.[\mathsf{tp}]y\;t)\;id\;\Omega,t\mathop{::}\mathsf{tp}\rangle

(
Push
-
Grab
)

\displaystyle\mapsto^{3}\langle\mu\beta.[\mathsf{tp}]id\;t,\Omega\mathop{::}t\mathop{::}\mathsf{tp}\rangle

(
Push
-
Push
-
Grab
)

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle id\;t,\mathsf{tp}\rangle

(
Capture
)

\displaystyle\mapsto^{2}\langle t,\mathsf{tp}\rangle

(
Push
-
Grab
)

After the first capture, the stack
t\mathop{::}\mathsf{tp}
is still in place,
while the stack
\Omega\mathop{::}t\mathop{::}\mathsf{tp}
disappears during the
second capture, because
\beta
does not occur in the body of the

\mu
operator.

Using a substitution-based semantics for the
\mu
operator means it
can easily be encoded in HOcore as a higher-order communication. We
translate names as HOcore variables, chosen to be distinct from the
set of translated term variables. When we evaluate closed terms, the
distinguished name
\mathsf{tp}
is the end of the stack and is translated as
such. The
\mu
operator is translated as follows.

\displaystyle\left\ldbrack\mathsf{tp}\right\rdbrack

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}\overline{b}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}}}

\displaystyle\left\ldbrack\mu\alpha.[\beta]t\right\rdbrack

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smalltriangleup}}{{=}}c(\alpha).(\left\ldbrack t\right\rdbrack\parallel\overline{c}{{\mathchoice{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\textstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}{{}\mathopen{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\langle\vphantom{\cdot}\hskip-1.2pt\right.$}}}}\mathclose{{{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left.\hskip-1.2pt\vphantom{\cdot}\right\rangle$}}}}}}})

The rest of the translation is the same as in
Section
3
. The translated
\mu
operator reflects the

Capture
step by receiving the current stack on
c
. Because
we execute closed terms, either
\beta=\alpha
, or
\beta
is bound
higher in the term and has been substituted before we get to this
point. The term
\left\ldbrack t\right\rdbrack
is therefore executed in a previously captured
stack.

The correspondence between the extended KAM and its HOcore translation can be stated the same way as for the regular KAM
(Theorem
1
). The
Capture
step
corresponds to exactly one communication in the translation of the machine.

7.2.
NFB Machine

To internalize normal-form bisimilarity into
the extended KAM, we need to distinguish not only free variables
f

from bound variables
x
, but also free names ,
\psi
from bound names

\delta
. Indeed, normal-form bisimilarity uses fresh names to compare
terms and discriminates terms based on these names. We change the
syntax of terms as follows.

\alpha\Coloneqq\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\delta\qquad t,s\Coloneqq f\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;x\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\lambda x.t\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;t\;s\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\mu\delta.[\alpha]t

We assume terms to be well-formed, i.e., to not contain a bound
variable or bound name without its binder. The name at the end of a
stack is necessarily free.

\pi\Coloneqq t\mathop{::}\pi\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;

Given two stacks
\pi_{1}
,
\pi_{2}
, we write
\pi_{1}\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}\pi_{2}
if
their elements are pairwise related and they are ended by the same
name. Formally, we have either
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}=
for some
, or
\pi_{1}=t\mathop{::}\pi^{\prime}_{1}
,
\pi_{2}=s\mathop{::}\pi^{\prime}_{2}
,

t\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}s
, and
\pi^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}\pi^{\prime}_{2}
. The definition of
normal-form bisimilarity for the call-by-name
\lambda\mu
-calculus is
as follows.

{defi}

A symmetric relation
\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}
is a normal-form bisimulation if
t\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}s
and fresh implies:

•

if
\langle t,\rangle\mathrel{{\mapsto}^{*}}\langle\lambda x.t^{\prime},\psi\rangle
, then there
exists
s^{\prime}
such that
\langle s,\rangle\mathrel{{\mapsto}^{*}}\langle\lambda x.s^{\prime},\psi\rangle
and
[f\mathbin{/}x]t^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}[f\mathbin{/}x]s^{\prime}

for a fresh
f
;

•

if
\langle t,\rangle\mathrel{{\mapsto}^{*}}\langle f,\pi\rangle
, then there
exists
\pi^{\prime}
such that
\langle s,\rangle\mathrel{{\mapsto}^{*}}\langle f,\pi^{\prime}\rangle
and
\pi\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}\pi^{\prime}
.

Normal-form bisimilarity is the largest normal-form bisimulation.

Normal-form bisimilarity is not complete w.r.t. contextual equivalence
of the
\lambda\mu
-calculus when defined either with weak-head
reduction
[
Las99a
]
or head reduction
[
Las06
]
. For
an extended
\lambda\mu
-calculus— the so-called
\Lambda\mu

calculus
[
Sau05
]
—with head reduction, it coincides with
solvable equivalence
[
Bar84
,
Las06
]
.

The bisimulation definition is almost the same as in

\lambda
-calculus (Definition
4.1
) except for
names. Terms are compared within some fresh name for each
bisimulation round, as using a single name like
\mathsf{tp}
for all rounds
would not be discriminating enough. It would relate for instance
x

and
\mu\delta.[\mathsf{tp}]x
, because
\langle x,\mathsf{tp}\rangle
and

\langle\mu\delta.[\mathsf{tp}]x,\mathsf{tp}\rangle
evaluate to
\langle x,\mathsf{tp}\rangle
, but they
behave differently: the second term discards its continuation while the
first one does not. Similarly, we expect the resulting normal forms to
exhibit a common name
\psi
(not necessarily equal to ). Terms
evaluating to normal forms with distinct toplevel names would imply
that they return to different continuations, and a surrounding context
could easily distinguish them by picking a terminating continuation
for a term and a diverging continuation for the other.

We adapt the
\lambda
-calculus NFB machine to take these changes into
account, resulting in the machine of Figure
11
. In the
machine, free names are natural numbers, and configurations

\langle t,\pi,n,m\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}
include an extra counter
m
to generate fresh
names. This counter is used as a new stack whenever we restart a
computation, in the
Lambda
and
Enter
steps. The

Lambda
and
Done
steps now flag the free name

f
of the resulting normal forms. The rest of the machine is the
same as in
\lambda
-calculus, except for the
Capture

step.

\displaystyle\langle t\;s,\pi,n,m\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle t,s\mathop{::}\pi,n,m\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Push
)

\displaystyle\langle\lambda x.t,s\mathop{::}\pi,n,m\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle[s\mathbin{/}x]t,\pi,n,m\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Grab
)

\displaystyle\langle\mu\delta.[\alpha]t,\pi,n,m\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\delta\rangle t,\langle\pi\mathbin{/}\delta\rangle\alpha,n,m\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Capture
)

\displaystyle\langle\lambda x.t,,n,m\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$$}}}\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\ocirc$}}}\langle[n\mathbin{/}x]t,m,n+1,m+1\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Lambda
)

\displaystyle\langle f,\pi,n,m\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$f$}}}\langle\pi,n,m\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

(
Var
)

\displaystyle\langle,n,m\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$$}}}\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\medstar$}}}

(
Done
)

\displaystyle\langle t\mathop{::}\pi,n,m\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\filledmedtriangledown$}}}\langle t,m,n,m+1\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Enter
)

\displaystyle\langle t\mathop{::}\pi,n,m\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xmapsto{\makebox[5.19876pt]{\scriptsize$\filledmedtriangleright$}}}\langle\pi,n,m\rangle_{\mathsf{ct}}

(
Skip
)

Figure 11.
NFB machine for the call-by-name
\lambda\mu
-calculus

The translation of the NFB machine for the
\lambda\mu
-calculus into
HOcore follows the same principles as in
\lambda
-calculus. We just
need to be careful to distinguish between numbers representing free
variables from those standing for free names—by using distinct
HOcore names
suc_{\mathsf{v}}
,
z_{\mathsf{v}}
and

suc_{\mathsf{n}}
,
z_{\mathsf{n}}
. We can prove the equivalence
between normal-form bisimilarity and its machine, and between the NFB
machine and its translation as in Section
4
.

Theorem 22
.

t\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{nf}}}s
iff there exists
n>\max{(\mathsf{fv}(t)\cup\mathsf{fv}(s))}
and

m>\max{(\mathsf{fn}(t)\cup\mathsf{fn}(s))}
such that

\left\ldbrack\langle t,m,n,m+1\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}\right\rdbrack\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle{HO}}}}\left\ldbrack\langle s,m,n,m+1\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}\right\rdbrack
.

7.3.
Environment-based Abstract Machine

As in

\lambda
-calculus (Section
5
),
internalizing applicative bisimilarity requires to generate a testing
argument. Because we cannot generate binders in the HOcore translation, we consider a de Bruijn syntax for terms and an
environment-based machine. However, unlike in
\lambda
-calculus,
named terms are not completely closed, because of the toplevel name,
and when we play the bisimulation game, considering a single free name

\mathsf{tp}
is not enough, as it would not be sound, like with normal-form
bisimilarity (cf Section
7.2
). We therefore need to
consider three distinct families of de Bruijn indices: term variables

n
, bound names
\delta
, and free names . The de Bruijn syntax
of the
\lambda\mu
-calculus is therefore as follows.

\alpha\Coloneqq\delta\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\qquad t,s\Coloneqq n\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\lambda.t\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;t\;s\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\mu.[\alpha]t

We present in Figure
12
the environment-based extended
KAM. It is similar to the Streicher and Reus
machine
[
SR98
]
, except their syntax uses names
and not de Bruijn indices, and they use a single environment for term
variables and bound names. Instead, we distinguish the environment
mapping term variables to closures (ranged by
e
,
d
) from
the one mapping bound names to stacks (ranged by
\Delta
,

\Gamma
). An extra environment mapping free names to stacks is added
when we internalize applicative bisimilarity
(Section
7.4
). Closures
\eta
are triples

(t,e,\Delta)
, and stacks
\pi
are composed of closures and
ended by a free or bound name.

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\eta\mathop{::}e\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\epsilon_{\mathsf{t}}

(term-variables environments)

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\pi\mathop{::}\Delta\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\epsilon_{\mathsf{b}}

(bound-names environments)

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle\Coloneqq(t,e,\Delta)

(closures)

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\eta\mathop{::}\pi\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\alpha

(stacks)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\langle t,e,\Delta,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\langle\delta,\Delta,\eta\rangle_{\mathsf{bn}}

(configurations)

\displaystyle\langle t\;s,e,\Delta,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle t,e,\Delta,(s,e,\Delta)\mathop{::}\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Push
)

\displaystyle\langle 0,(t,e,\Delta)\mathop{::}d,\Gamma,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle t,e,\Delta,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Zero-Term
)

\displaystyle\langle n+1,\eta\mathop{::}e,\Delta,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle n,e,\Delta,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Env-Term
)

\displaystyle\langle\lambda.t,e,\Delta,\eta\mathop{::}\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle t,\eta\mathop{::}e,\Delta,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Grab
)

\displaystyle\langle\mu.[\alpha]t,e,\Delta,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle t,e,\pi\mathop{::}\Delta,\alpha\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Capture
)

\displaystyle\langle\lambda.t,e,\Delta,\delta\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle\delta,\Delta,(\lambda.t,e,\Delta)\rangle_{\mathsf{bn}}

(
Restore
)

\displaystyle\langle 0,\pi\mathop{::}\Gamma,(t,e,\Delta)\rangle_{\mathsf{bn}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle t,e,\Delta,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Zero-BName
)

\displaystyle\langle\delta+1,\pi\mathop{::}\Delta,\eta\rangle_{\mathsf{bn}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle\delta,\Delta,\eta\rangle_{\mathsf{bn}}

(
Env-BName
)

Figure 12.
Environment-based KAM for the
\lambda\mu
-calculus

The first four steps are as in the
\lambda
-calculus. The

Capture
step replaces the current stack
\pi
with

\alpha
and puts
\pi
on top of the bound-name environment

\Delta
. Whenever a
\lambda
-abstraction is executed within a stack
composed only of a bound name
\delta
, we restore the previously
captured stack, by looking into
\Delta
. It is the role of the

Zero-BName
and
Env-BName
steps, which operate on
dedicated configurations of the form
\langle\delta,\Delta,\eta\rangle_{\mathsf{bn}}
.

7.4.
AB machine

The definition of the sound and complete applicative bisimilarity for
the
\lambda\mu
-calculus
[
BL14
]
is more
intricate than in
\lambda
-calculus, because we compare named
terms. We first recall its definition
[
BL14
]

and using named binders, before moving to de Bruijn indices, and then
to an AB machine.

7.4.1.
Informal definition with named binders

Assume two
named terms
[\alpha]t
and
[\alpha]s
where
t
and
s
are
closed. If they evaluate to respectively
[\alpha]\lambda x.t^{\prime}
and

[\alpha]\lambda x.s^{\prime}
, a surrounding context cannot test them by
applying them to an arbitrary closed term
t^{\prime\prime}
, as

([\alpha]\lambda x.t^{\prime})\;t^{\prime\prime}
is not a valid

\lambda\mu
-term. The simplest named term that can be built to lead
to such an application is

[\beta](\mu\alpha.[\alpha]\lambda x.t^{\prime})\;t^{\prime\prime}
for a fresh

\beta
. Indeed, the
\mu
-binder captures the named context

[\beta]\square\;t^{\prime\prime}
(represented as a stack

t^{\prime\prime}\mathop{::}\beta
) to reduce to

[\beta](\lambda x.\langle t^{\prime\prime}\mathop{::}\beta\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle t^{\prime})\;t^{\prime\prime}
; the structural substitution
\langle t^{\prime\prime}\mathop{::}\beta\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle t^{\prime}

is necessary because
\alpha
may occur in
t^{\prime}
. The
\beta
-reduction
is now possible and the previous named term reduces to

[\beta][t^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{/}x]\langle t^{\prime\prime}\mathop{::}\beta\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle t^{\prime}
. We get the same sequence of reductions with
[\alpha]s
, so that applicative bisimilarity compares

[\beta][t^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{/}x]\langle t^{\prime\prime}\mathop{::}\beta\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle t^{\prime}\mbox{ and }[\beta][t^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{/}x]\langle t^{\prime\prime}\mathop{::}\beta\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle s^{\prime}.

We need to write such a test with de Bruijn indices and
environments. We represent the successive fresh names
\alpha
,

\beta
,
\ldots
used by the bisimilarity with indices 0, 1,

\ldots
Assume we evaluate the closed closures
(t,e,\Delta)

and
(s,d,\Gamma)
in the free name
0
, resulting respectively
into
(\lambda.t^{\prime},e^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
and

(\lambda.s^{\prime},d^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime})
in the same name
0
. Let
t^{\prime\prime}
be a
closed term. The term substitution
[t^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{/}x]\cdot
is
represented as in plain
\lambda
-calculus, by extending
e^{\prime}
and

d^{\prime}
with the closure corresponding to
t^{\prime\prime}
. To represent the
structural substitution
\langle t^{\prime\prime}\mathop{::}\beta\mathbin{/}\alpha\rangle\cdot
we
introduce an extra environment dedicated to free names.

7.4.2.
Testing environments

A
free-names environment

\Phi
is a mapping from free names
(represented as de Bruijn indices) to stacks, defined as follows.

\Phi\Coloneqq\pi\mathop{::}\Phi\;\;\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}\;\;\epsilon_{\mathsf{f}}

This environment is extended by the bisimilarity each time it tests
pairs of named values. We write
\ulcorner t^{\prime\prime}\urcorner
for the closure

(t^{\prime\prime},\epsilon_{\mathsf{t}},\epsilon_{\mathsf{b}},\epsilon_{\mathsf{f}})
used in these tests. The stack

t^{\prime\prime}\mathop{::}\beta
is represented as
\ulcorner t^{\prime\prime}\urcorner\mathop{::}1
, and

\Phi
maps
0
to
\ulcorner t^{\prime\prime}\urcorner\mathop{::}1
. A subsequent test would
extend
\Phi
to map
1
to
\ulcorner t^{\prime\prime\prime}\urcorner\mathop{::}2
for some
t^{\prime\prime\prime}
,
then a following test would map
2
to another closure,
etc.

We see that the bisimilarity extends
\Phi
at the end, and we write

\Phi\mathop{:::}\pi
for such an extension. We also notice that the
resulting
\Phi
maps each free name to a stack containing only one
element. More precisely, an environment
\Phi
generated by
successive bisimilarity tests is either empty, or its
i^{\text{th}}

element is of the form
\ulcorner t\urcorner\mathop{::}i
for some closed
term
t
. We say such an environment is a
testing environment
,
of rank if it has elements. A testing environment of
rank
>0
has only as free-name index without a mapping, at
the end of its last stack. For example, a testing environment of rank
2 is of the form
\pi_{0}\mathop{::}\pi_{1}\mathop{::}\epsilon_{\mathsf{f}}
, mapping
0
to

\pi_{0}=\ulcorner t_{0}\urcorner\mathop{::}1
for some closed
t_{0}
, and
1
to

\pi_{1}=\ulcorner t_{1}\urcorner\mathop{::}2
for some closed
t_{1}
.

7.4.3.
Applicative bisimilarity

We extend closures to include testing environments

\eta\Coloneqq(t,e,\Delta,\Phi)
, and we say that
\eta
is of
rank when its testing environment is of rank . A stack is
closed if it is composed only of closed closures. A closure is closed
if
e
contains more elements than the highest term-variable index
in
t
,
\Delta
contains more elements than the highest bound-name
index in
t
,
e
contains only closed closures, and
\Delta
only
closed stacks.

We add the testing environment
\Phi
to configurations, and we
define steps to look in
\Phi
for the stack corresponding to a given
free-name index. We discuss these changes in details when defining the AB
machine. We adapt the definition of applicative
bisimilarity
[
BL14
]
to our setting as
follows.

{defi}

A symmetric relation
\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}
on closed closures is an applicative
bisimulation if

(t,e,\Delta,\Phi)\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}(s,d,\Gamma,\Phi)
with

\Phi
of rank and

\langle t,e,\Delta,\Phi,\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}\mathrel{{\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}}^{*}}\langle\lambda.t^{\prime},e^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},\Phi,\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}
implies that there exists
s^{\prime}
,

d^{\prime}
, and
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that

\langle s,d,\Gamma,\Phi,\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}\mathrel{{\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}}^{*}}\langle\lambda.s^{\prime},d^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime},\Phi,\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}
and for all closed
t^{\prime\prime}
, we
have

(t^{\prime},\ulcorner t^{\prime\prime}\urcorner\mathop{::}e^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},\Phi\mathop{:::}(\ulcorner t^{\prime\prime}\urcorner\mathop{::}+1))\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}\\
(s^{\prime},\ulcorner t^{\prime\prime}\urcorner\mathop{::}d^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime},\Phi\mathop{:::}(\ulcorner t^{\prime\prime}\urcorner\mathop{::}+1)).

Applicative bisimilarity
\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{app}}}
is the largest applicative bisimulation.

Applicative bisimilarity executes closed closures with the same
testing environment of rank within the stack composed only of
the free name . It then compares the resulting values by
considering a closed closure
\ulcorner t^{\prime\prime}\urcorner
to instantiate the

\lambda
-abstractions, and to extend the testing environment which
becomes of rank
+1
.

7.4.4.
AB machine

We present the most interesting steps of
the corresponding AB machine in Figure
13
. Evaluation
configurations
\langle t,e,\Delta,\Phi,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}
now include the
testing environment
\Phi
, which is passed along unchanged in the
steps corresponding to the extended KAM (omitted in
Figure
13
).

Whenever a
\lambda
-abstraction is executed in a stack composed only
of a free name , we have two possibilities: either is
mapped in
\Phi
, otherwise we have to generate the closure
\ulcorner t\urcorner
of the bisimulation test. The configuration
\langle,\Phi,\eta,\Phi_{r},^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathsf{fn}}
is designed with these two cases in mind. Its
main role is to look for in
\Phi
and to restore the
corresponding stack if the mapping exists.

\displaystyle\langle t\;s,e,\Delta,\Phi,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle t,e,\Delta,\Phi,(s,e,\Delta,\Phi)\mathop{::}\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Push
)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.99994pt\vdots

(cf. Figure
12
)

\displaystyle\langle\lambda.t,e,\Delta,\Phi,\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle,\Phi,(\lambda.t,e,\Delta,\Phi),\epsilon_{\mathsf{f}},\rangle_{\mathsf{fn}}

(
Lookup-FName
)

\displaystyle\langle 0,\pi\mathop{::}\Phi^{\prime},(t,e,\Delta,\Phi),\Phi_{r},\rangle_{\mathsf{fn}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle t,e,\Delta,\Phi,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Zero-FName
)

\displaystyle\langle+1,\pi\mathop{::}\Phi,\eta,\Phi_{r},^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathsf{fn}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}}\langle,\Phi,\eta,\pi\mathop{::}{\Phi_{r}},^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathsf{fn}}

(
Env-FName
)

\displaystyle\langle 0,\epsilon_{\mathsf{f}},\eta,\Phi_{r},\rangle_{\mathsf{fn}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\ocirc$}}}\langle 0,1,0,\odot,\eta,\Phi_{r},\rangle_{\mathsf{ind}}

(
Arg
)

\displaystyle\langle n,\kappa,\kappa_{\mu},\rho,\eta,\Phi_{r},\rangle_{\mathsf{ind}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\boxplus$}}}\langle n+1,\kappa+1,\kappa_{\mu},\rho,\eta,\Phi_{r},\rangle_{\mathsf{ind}}

(
Suc
)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.99994pt\vdots

(cf. Figure
4
)

\displaystyle\langle t,\kappa,\kappa_{\mu},\rho,\eta,\Phi_{r},\rangle_{\mathsf{tm}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$i\mu$}}}\langle 0,t,\kappa,\kappa_{\mu},\rho,\eta,\Phi_{r},\rangle_{\mathsf{indMu}}

(
Ind-Mu
)

\displaystyle\langle\delta,t,\kappa,\kappa_{\mu},\rho,\eta,\Phi_{r},\rangle_{\mathsf{indMu}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$s\mu$}}}\langle\delta+1,t,\kappa,\kappa_{\mu},\rho,\eta,\Phi_{r},\rangle_{\mathsf{indMu}}

(
Suc-Mu
)

\displaystyle\langle\delta,t,\kappa,\kappa_{\mu},\rho,\eta,\Phi_{r},\rangle_{\mathsf{indMu}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\mu$}}}\langle\mu.[\delta]t,\kappa,\mathsf{max}(\delta+1,\kappa_{\mu})-1,\rho,\eta,\Phi_{r},\rangle_{\mathsf{tm}}

(
Mu
)

\displaystyle\langle t,0,0,\odot,\eta,\Phi_{r},\rangle_{\mathsf{tm}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\textsuperscript{\textregistered}$}}}\langle\Phi_{r},(\ulcorner t\urcorner\mathop{::}+1)\mathop{::}\epsilon_{\mathsf{f}},\eta,\ulcorner t\urcorner\mathop{::}+1\rangle_{\mathsf{envfn}}

(
Init-Rev
)

\displaystyle\langle\pi\mathop{::}{\Phi_{r}},\Phi,\eta,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{envfn}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\textsuperscript{\textregistered}$}}}\langle\Phi_{r},\pi\mathop{::}\Phi,\eta,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{envfn}}

(
Rev
)

\displaystyle\langle\epsilon_{\mathsf{f}},\Phi,(\lambda.s,e,\Delta,\Phi^{\prime}),\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{envfn}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\xmapsto{\makebox[12.51709pt]{\scriptsize$\medstar$}}}\langle\lambda.s,e,\Delta,\Phi,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}

(
Restart
)

Figure 13.
AB machine for the call-by-name
\lambda\mu
-calculus

If is not mapped, then it is stored as the last parameter of
the
\mathsf{fn}
mode in the
Lookup-FName
step, and passed
along during the argument generation process. Its successor is needed
to create the stack
\ulcorner t\urcorner\mathop{::}{+1}
(
Init-Rev

step).

The second-to-last parameter
\Phi_{r}
of the
\mathsf{fn}
mode is
used to compute the reverse of
\Phi
. Indeed, we remind that
applicative bisimilarity extends
\Phi
from its tail. To do so, we
reverse
\Phi
, add the generated closure, and reverse the result
again. We set
\Phi_{r}
to empty in the
Lookup-FName
step,
and then the elements of
\Phi
are pushed on
\Phi_{r}
when we go
through
\Phi
in the
Env-FName
step. As a result, when

\Phi
is empty in the
Arg
transition,
\Phi_{r}
contains
the original environment in reverse.

The
Arg
step initiates the argument generation. It triggers
when
\Phi
is empty, and we know that the index at this point is

0
, because the only free-name index without a mapping allowed by
design is the rank of
\Phi
, i.e., its size. As in
Section
5.2
, we start argument generation with the
leftmost de Bruijn index representing a term variable, and then
generate the term from left to right. Compared to
\lambda
-calculus,
the
\mathsf{ind}
and
\mathsf{tm}
configurations carry three extra
parameters: the already discussed
\Phi_{r}
and , and

\kappa_{\mu}
, which counts how many
\mu
-binders are necessary for the
generated term to be closed. In Figure
13
, we omit the
steps that are the same as in Figure
4
up to these three
parameters.

We generate a term
\mu.[\delta]t
by starting with the bound name index

\delta
(
Ind-Mu
and
Suc-Mu
steps). We then add the

\mu
-binder and update the
\kappa_{\mu}
counter accordingly
(
Mu
step).

After generating the argument
t
, we step to a configuration of the
form
\langle\Phi_{r},\Phi,\eta,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{envfn}}
, the goal of which is to
construct the extended testing environment. The
Init-Rev

step constructs the bottom of the extended environment and remembers

\ulcorner t\urcorner\mathop{::}+1
as the new stack. The
Rev
step
goes through
\Phi_{r}
and pushes its elements on
\Phi
to
reconstruct the original environment on top of its new bottom. When

\Phi_{r}
is empty, the
Restart
step restarts the machine,
executing
\lambda.s
in the stack
\ulcorner t\urcorner\mathop{::}+1
: the next
step is a
\beta
-reduction which produces the configuration we want.

7.4.5.
Full abstraction

As in
\lambda
-calculus, the AB
machine flags each step of its argument generation process, and for
each generated argument
t
there is a corresponding sequence of flags

\mathsf{Seq}(t)
. We can prove as in Section
5.2
that
applicative bisimilarity and the AB machine equivalence
coincide. Besides, the AB machine relies on ingredients and operations
(counters, max,
etc.
) that we know how to translate in HOcore in a
faithful and deterministic way (see Appendix
A
), and for
which we can prove the
following full abstraction theorem.

Theorem 23
.

For all closed closures
(t,e,\Delta,\Phi)
and
(s,d,\Gamma,\Phi)
of rank ,
(t,e,\Delta,\Phi)\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{app}}}(s,d,\Gamma,\Phi)
iff
\left\ldbrack\langle t,e,\Delta,\Phi,\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}\right\rdbrack\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle{HO}}}}\left\ldbrack\langle s,d,\Gamma,\Phi,\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}\right\rdbrack
.

Full abstraction can also be achieved by internalizing contextual
equivalence directly. The resulting machine would extend the machine
of Figure
6
with the steps generating
\mu
-binders of
Figure
13
.

8.
Conclusion and Future Work

We propose encodings of the call-by-name and call-by-value

\lambda
-calculus with or without control operators into HOcore,
fully abstract w.r.t. normal-form and applicative bisimilarities, and
contextual equivalence. This shows that a minimal higher-order
calculus with a fixed number of hidden names, which is much less
expressive than the name-passing
\pi
-calculus, still has enough
expressive power to faithfully encode these calculi.

We use abstract machines not only to fix the reduction
strategy, but also as an intermediary step between the

\lambda
-calculus and HOcore. We turn the equivalences of the

\lambda
-calculus, and their potentially complex testing conditions,
into a first-order bisimilarity over an LTS (a flag-generating
machine), which is closer to the HOcore equivalence. We believe this
internalization technique can be applied to any language for which an
abstract machine has been defined. No matter how intricate the
bisimilarities for such a language are, it should be always possible
to generate a context as in Section
5.4
to internalize
contextual equivalence.

The encodings of the extended abstract machines into HOcore rely on
the same principles, e.g., to represent stacks, non-deterministic
choice, case analyses on terms,
etc.
We believe it is possible to
automatically derive the encoding from an abstract machine so that
the generated translation verify Lemmas
6

and
8
, giving us Theorem
12
for free.

Our encodings are
weak

compositional
[
Gor10
,
Par06
]
, i.e., they consist of
a compositional translation of the
\lambda
-term inside a fixed
process representing the machine. We conjecture there is no fully
abstract compositional encoding of the
\lambda
-calculus into
HOcore. In such an encoding, a translated
\lambda
-abstraction could
be composed with an argument (a translated
\lambda
-term) to

\beta
-reduce. It would require at least one communication on a
public channel name, but the
\lambda
-abstraction should still be
able to protect itself from unwanted behaviours from the outside. It
seems difficult if not impossible to achieve with only hidden names
and no name restriction.

As demonstrated in other
settings
[
Pre22
,
JS22
]
, our encodings
could be useful to import proof techniques such as up-to
techniques
[
SR12
]
from the process calculus world into the

\lambda
-calculus world. It would be interesting especially for
applicative bisimilarity for which powerful up-to techniques have not
been defined yet.

Finally, we would like to explore further the expressiveness of
process calculi by translating the
\lambda
-calculus without a
predefined reduction strategy. The encodings defined so far in the
literature (cf. Section
1
) assumes a given strategy for
the
\lambda
-calculus. An exception is the work by Cai and
Fu
[
CF11
]
, where a
\lambda
-term is represented as a
tree, and
\beta
-reduction is a transformation on trees. It relies on
an unbounded number of restricted names to represent tree nodes; we
wonder if we can use the same ideas with only a fixed number of
names. However, such an encoding works for any source language, as it
is a manipulation of syntax trees. We would like the encoding to be
more tailored to the
\lambda
-calculus, to tell us more about the
relationship between the
\lambda
-calculus and process calculi.

Appendix A
Translation of the
\lambda\mu
-calculus
AB Machine into HOcore

The translation of the AB machine for the call-by-name

\lambda\mu
-calculus extends the one for the plain

\lambda
-calculus of Section
5.3
. We omit the
parts of the translation which are the same or similar to the plain

\lambda
-calculus one.

The translation of the evaluation mode is as follows. A closure is now
composed of four messages. Because the closures are supposed to be
closed, the process representing
the empty environments
\epsilon_{\mathsf{t}}
and
\epsilon_{\mathsf{b}}
should never be executed, and we choose
to represent them with
{\boldsymbol{0}}
. The definitions of
\left\ldbrack\epsilon_{\mathsf{f}}\right\rdbrack
and
\left\ldbrack\alpha\right\rdbrack
are
discussed later.

\displaystyle\left\ldbrack\langle t,e,\Delta,\Phi,\pi\rangle_{\mathsf{ev}}\right\rdbrack

If the search fails, it means that is not mapped to a stack, and
\left\ldbrack\epsilon_{\mathsf{f}}\right\rdbrack
should start generating the argument. It first discards the
message on
sv_{\mathsf{f}}
which is no longer useful and sets up the

\mathsf{ind}
mode.

The message on
km
is for the counter
\kappa_{\mu}
, and the
translation
\left\ldbrack\kappa_{\mu}\right\rdbrack_{\mathsf{cm}}
is the same as
\left\ldbrack\kappa\right\rdbrack_{\mathsf{c}}
, but
using different names
zerom
and
sucm
. The
\mathsf{RecTm}
process
generating terms needs an extra case analysis on
\kappa_{\mu}
, as the
term generation process may end only if
\kappa_{\mu}=0
. The process

\mathsf{RecTm}
of Section
5.3
is modified to do this case
analysis when
\kappa=0
.

Generating a de Bruijn index for a term variable, a

\lambda
-abstraction, or an application is done the same way as in
plain
\lambda
-calculus: the processes
\mathsf{RecInd}
,
\mathsf{Succ}
,
\mathsf{Var}
,

\mathsf{Lambda}
,
\mathsf{AppFun}
, and
\mathsf{App}
are the same as in
Section
5.3
.

The process
\mathsf{Mu}
generates a
\mu
binder, starting with the bound
name
\delta
, simulating the
\mathsf{indMu}
mode. It initiates a
counter on
km2
, which is increased in parallel with
\delta
. That
counter is then compared in
\mathsf{MuTm}
with the one on
km
to compute
the maximum between them, using the same technique as in
\mathsf{App}
but
on different names. The processes
\mathsf{RecIMu}
and
\mathsf{SuccMu}
behave like
their respective counterpart
\mathsf{RecInd}
and
\mathsf{Succ}
.

The process
\mathsf{DoneTm}
is executed when we are done computing the term

t
and start computing the new testing environment
\Phi
. Using the process
representing saved on the name
sv
, it computes the stack
at the bottom of this new environment
\ulcorner t\urcorner\mathop{::}+1
, saves
it on the name
sv_{\pi}
, and put it also on the name
newf
on which

\Phi
is computed.
